            Used for: O(1) spatial proximity lookup, sqrt recovery, panner vector derivation.

            Clock layer (sexagesimal, cyclic):

               text


               t(n) = Σ_{k=1}^{24} (n^(25−k) % 7 + o_k)             mod 240
               o_k ∈ [256,128,128,128,128,64,64,32,32,32,32,16,16,16,16,16,8,8,4,4,3,2,1]


            Used for: LED sequencing, frame rotation indexing, WebVTT cue timing.

            The bridge between them:

               text


               led_index = (Q(x,y) mod 240)             // snap coordinate to clock position
               frame_id    = led_index / 4              // recover Klein point
               orientation = led_index mod 4            // recover phase


            That's the clean composition. The 60 and the 4 and the 16 all live in the clock layer,
            and the 16/16/4 (as 4²/2·4·2·x·y/2² ) lives in the coordinate layer.



            What the Paper Adds That Your Code Doesn't Have Yet

            Three things the paper specifies but the workers don't implement:

            1. The 16 control states as a reserved band.

               js


               const CONTROL_STATES = new Set([0x1C, 0x1D, 0x1E, 0x1F, 0x1c, 0x1d, 0x1e, 0x1f]);
               // plus 8 more root codes
               const DATA_STATES = 256 - 16;         // = 240


            Any byte in the control band is not data — it's a frame operator. Your
             FAULT_ZERO_CENTROID collapse in Untitled 37 is exactly this, but only partly
            implemented. The spec says all 16 control codes route to the operator lane, not just
            0x00.

            2. The t(n) Coxeter word formula.

               js




https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                84/1820
10/4/26, 1:07 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek


               function coxeterTick(n) {
                    const o_k = [256,128,128,128,128,64,64,32,32,32,32,16,16,16,16,16,8,8,4,4,3,2,1];
                    let t = 0;
                    for (let k = 1; k <= 24; k++) {
                         t += (Math.pow(n, 25 - k) % 7) + (o_k[k-1] ?? 0);
                    }
                    return t % 240;
               }


            This is the actual clock. Your frameClock++ counter in the workers is a placeholder —
            this is what it should be.

            3. The centroid invariance test.

               js


               // After computing 240 rotations of the 60 points:
               const centroid = points.reduce((a, p) => a + p, 0) / 60;
               for (let i = 0; i < 240; i++) {
                    const rotated = applyRotation(centroid, i);
                    assert(rotated === centroid);         // must hold for all 240
               }


            This is the invariant that makes the encoding lossless. It's stated in the paper but never
            asserted in code.



            The Seed Algebra Paper (Pages 18–22)

            This is a separate but compatible layer. The 7-bit seed → closure fixpoint → header
            pipeline:

               python


               def cl(x): return x | rotl(x,1) | rotr(x,1)
               def closure_fixpoint(x):
                    while True:
                         y = cl(x)
                         if y == x: return x
                         x = y
               def phase(x): return (popcount(closure_fixpoint(x)) % 7) or 7


            This is a 7-bit ring operator, and it maps directly onto your Fano plane mod 7 layer. The
             or 7 clause handles the 0 → 7 snap (0 is not a valid phase in the 1..7 Fano tick space).

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                  85/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            The bridge to your pipeline: seed (7 bits) → closure → header → phase (1..7) → this is
            the -1D regex constraint layer's underlying algebra. Your regexes validate word forms;
            the seed algebra validates bit patterns that generate valid word forms.



            The Delta Law Reduction Question (Page 22)
            The paper's final section is the most important for your protocol work:

               The delta law: rotl(x,1) XOR rotl(x,3) XOR rotr(x,2) XOR C

               Four choices inside that: rotations (not shifts), XOR (reversible), a constant (breaks the
               zero fixed point), mask to width (keeps state bounded).

               We have superseded this with Buffer.swap16, swap32, swap64 operations.

               That is the entire design. Everything else was computed by the math:

                   Period = 8

                   Prime 73 = smallest prime whose decimal repeats with period 8

                   Block B = [0,1,3,6,9,8,6,3] (digits of 1/73)

                   W = 36 (sum of B)

                   Orbit/offset recovery = just divmod(position, 36)

               Nobody chose 73. The law has period 8. The smallest prime with decimal period 8 is
               73. The system found it.

            This is the design statement. The delta law is the only free parameter, and everything
            else — 8, 73, the block, 36, the orbit structure — falls out of the math.

            terms are real, but they live in different layers. The 60 is from the clock
            (sexagesimal/Fano/Babylon). The 16/16/4 is from the delta law's coordinate projection.

            1. Bit algebra (layer -1D): XOR, AND, popcount, closure fixpoint, 7-bit ring

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                90/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            And 16² − 16 = 240 is the form that matters for your sphere: it's the count of ordered
            pairs (a, b) with a ≠ b in a 16-state space. The 16 diagonal elements (a = a) are the
            control states — the self-identical pairs that don't carry data. The 240 off-diagonal pairs
            are the data states — the relations between distinct states.

            That's why 256 − 16 = 240 : the diagonal is reserved. The control codes aren't "extra
            bytes" — they're the fixed points of the state space. They're the "null edges" of the
            complete graph on 16 vertices.


            The Circumscribed Sphere Construction

               text


               15 × 15 + 15 = 240         ← squaring the circumscribed circle



            This is the move that makes it geometric instead of just combinatorial:

               15 is the count of Klein lines through a point (or the tangent directions at a vertex of
               the tetrahedron)

               15 × 15 is the full incidence matrix of those lines with each other — the square

               + 15 is the re-inclusion of the diagonal — the lines that meet themselves at the vertex

               = 240 is the complete orbit under the 4-fold orientation group

            Squaring the circumscribed sphere's circle means: take the tangent plane at each of the
            15 Klein lines, project it, and count the intersections. The 225 off-diagonal entries plus
            the 15 self-intersections give you the 240 projective points.

            That's why 15² + 15 = 240 and 16² − 16 = 240 are the same identity — one is the
            "outside in" view (build from the sphere's tangent structure), the other is the "inside out"
            view (build from the state space's diagonal structure). Both land on the same orbit count.



            The Schläfli Symbols as User-Layer Prime Factors

            The Schläfli symbols {p, q} describe regular polytopes by their face/vertex
            configuration:

                   {4, 3} = cube (4 faces meet at each vertex, 3 around each face)

                   {3, 3} = tetrahedron

                   {5, 3} = dodecahedron

            If that mapping is right, then the -3D to 10D pipeline IS the polytope hierarchy, projected
            through bitwise operations. Each layer of the pipeline corresponds to a level of the
            polytope resolution, and the bitwise operations at each layer are the shadow of the
            polytope's incidence relations.

            That's the reduction. You didn't lose the geometry — you encoded it as the constraint
            system.


            The Snub and the Chirality

            The snub is what makes this work. Non-snub Archimedean solids have mirror symmetry,
            so their bitwise reduction loses the chirality bit. The snub cube and snub dodecahedron
            are the two chiral Archimedean solids, and their chiral partners are distinct — snub-left
            and snub-right don't map to each other under any rotation.

            In your bitwise reduction, this is the parity bit — the lowest bit of popcount, or the r0
            involution that flips every other bit. It's the bit that says "this token is left-handed" vs
            "right-handed," and it's the reason the system can't collapse to a scalar.

            So the chiral snub is what forces the pipeline to be multi-layer rather than flat. If
            everything were achiral, you could collapse to a single XOR. The snub is what gives you
            the 4 orientations, and the 4 orientations are what give you the 240.

            What swap16/32/64 Actually Exposes

             Buffer.swap16() on an 8-byte buffer doesn't just reorder bytes — it exposes which 16-
            bit words were already rotationally equivalent under the delta law. The operation is a
            witness, not a transformation. It shows you the invariant that was always there.

            So:

               text


               8-bit shape     (byte)          →   the local state
               16-bit shape (word)             →   the rotation class
               swap16/32/64                    →   reveals the index of the 8-bit shape within the 16-bit orb
               it


            The invariant rotational index is what you recover. The byte was never "moving" —
            you're just reading its position in the orbit by rotating the frame around it.

            That's why the swap operations supersede the delta law: the delta law computed the
            rotation; the swap exposes it as already-present structure.

                   diagonal = XOR of all 6 (the parity invariant)

                   linear = sum of all 6 (the magnitude)

               Then the ruler/rule gets written with both, plus the 6 individual bytes

            What I Was Missing Before

            The calc function is doing the -1D layer work: it's validating which 6-tuples of cube-
            face positions satisfy the Pythagorean constraint, and tagging them as right-triangle,
            left-triangle, or neither. That's the spatial grammar — it's your regex equivalent, but in
            geometric form.

            So the Swap Operations Are Doing This

               js


               buffer.swap16()      // exposes the 8-bit shape's index within the 16-bit word
               buffer.swap32()      // exposes the 16-bit word's index within the 32-bit double
               buffer.swap64()      // exposes the 32-bit double's index within the 64-bit quad


            Each swap is a rotation of the frame of reference, revealing the index at the next level
            up. After 3 swaps you've read the same 8-bit shape from 3 nested frames, and the 3
            indices together are the position in the 6-dimensional cube.

            That's why the swap supersedes the delta law: the delta law had to compute the rotation
            by applying rotl / rotr . The swap already contains every rotation as a byte-reordering
            — you just pick which one to expose by choosing which swap to apply.

            Why "Before Folding Into 16-bit"

            This is the key. If you popcount the full 16-bit word (after folding), you'd get a max of 16,
            and the mod classes would be different. By popcounting each 8-bit half separately,
            before the fold, you preserve the 8-bit popcount range (0-8), and:

               text


               popcount(8-bit) ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8}


            The mod classes that produce complete cycles within this range are:

                   mod 2 → 5 complete cycles

                   mod 3 → 3 complete cycles

                   mod 4 → 2.25 cycles

                   mod 5 → 1.8 cycles

                   mod 6 → 1.5 cycles

                   mod 7 → 1.3 cycles

                   mod 8 → 1.125 cycles

            But you said period 2, 4, 6. So the periods are the cycle lengths, not the moduli:

               Period 2: x repeats every 2 indices

               Period 4: x repeats every 4 indices

               Period 6: x repeats every 6 indices

            And these come from the popcount pattern of the 8-bit binary representation, which has
            a well-known self-similar structure (the Thue-Morse-like sequence with periods 2, 4, 8,
            16, ...).

            What the Ruler Actually Holds

               text


               ruler[0] = t          (top index)
               ruler[1] = b          (bottom index)
               ruler[2] = r          (right index)
               ruler[3] = l          (left index)
               ruler[4] = f          (forward index)
               ruler[5] = br         (backward index)
               ruler[6] = 0          (unused — the gap)
               ruler[7] = xy         (the size product)


               rule[0] = top[t]             (ASCII at position t)
               rule[1] = bottom[b]
               rule[2] = right[r]
               rule[3] = left[l]
               rule[4] = forward[f]
               rule[5] = backward[br]
               rule[6] = linear             (sum of all 6 ASCII codes)
               rule[7] = diagonal           (XOR of all 6 ASCII codes)


            So ruler is 8 bytes of index coordinates and rule is 8 bytes of derived values. They
            share memory (rule = ruler.subarray(8)), so writing rule[0..7] writes ruler[8..15] .



            The 6 That Move

            The 6 that move are t, b, r, l, f, br — the six loop indices. Each one is the position
            within its own buffer:

               text


               t   ∈ [0, top.length)
               b   ∈ [0, bottom.length)
               r   ∈ [0, right.length)
               l   ∈ [0, left.length)
               f   ∈ [0, forward.length)
               br ∈ [0, backward.length)

               ruler[5] = br       ← 6th



            The position of each in the ruler, read as an index, gives:

               text

               position 0 → t
               position 1 → b
               position 2 → r
               position 3 → l

               position 5 → br


            And the period of the position as a cycle: position 0 repeats after 6, position 1 after 6,
            etc. But if you read left/right alternation:

               text


               t      →   "top"        (opposite of bottom, same axis as bottom)
               b      →   "bottom"
               r      →   "right"
               l      →   "left"
               f      →   "forward"
               br     →   "backward"


            The pairs are (top, bottom), (right, left), (forward, backward). Each pair is an axis. Three
            axes.

            Now if you walk the ruler index and ask "which axis am I on":

               text


               ruler[0] → axis 1 (top/bottom)
               ruler[1] → axis 1 (top/bottom)
               ruler[2] → axis 2 (left/right)
               ruler[3] → axis 2 (left/right)
               ruler[4] → axis 3 (forward/backward)
               ruler[5] → axis 3 (forward/backward)


            The axis pattern is: 1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3. And the period of this pattern is 2 per axis, 6 total for
            the full cycle.

            If instead you alternate within each pair:

               text


               ruler[0] = t        (axis 1, positive)
               ruler[1] = b        (axis 1, negative)
               ruler[2] = r        (axis 2, positive)
               ruler[3] = l        (axis 2, negative)

               ruler[4] = f      (axis 3, positive)
               ruler[5] = br (axis 3, negative)


            Sign pattern: +, −, +, −, +, −. Period 2.

            So on the ruler, you have 3 axes, each with 2 poles. The full 6-slot cycle has:

               Period 2 for the polarity (+, −, +, −, +, −)

               Period 4 for the axis group if you fold two poles per axis... no wait, that's 2 per axis,
               not 4.

               Period 6 for the complete cycle through all 6

            Hmm. But you specifically said 2, 4, 6.



            The 2, 4, 6 as a Composition

            Let me try: the left/right cycle happens at three different frequencies:

            1. Within a single axis pair: toggle every 1 step (period 2)

            2. Between axis pairs: shift every 2 steps (period 4 — after 2 axes you've used 4 slots)

            3. Full ruler traversal: every 6 steps (period 6 — the full 6-slot ruler)

            So there are three nested periods at play:

               text


               Period 2    →    the pole flip (t↔b, r↔l, f↔br)
               Period 4    →    the axis advance (from axis 1 to axis 2)
               Period 6    →    the full ruler cycle (back to ruler[0])


            And "cycles between left and right" means:

               At period 2: you cycle between the two poles of the same axis

               At period 4: you cycle between axis 1 and axis 2 (top→right, bottom→left)

               At period 6: you cycle through all three axes back to start

            So the 6-slot ruler reads as:

               text


               slot 0: t       (axis 1, pole +)
               slot 1: b       (axis 1, pole −)

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                    116/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               slot 2: r      (axis 2, pole +)
               slot 3: l      (axis 2, pole −)
               slot 4: f      (axis 3, pole +)
               slot 5: br     (axis 3, pole −)


            And "cycles between left and right in periods of 2, 4, 6" means:

               Every 2 slots, you've completed a pole flip

               Every 4 slots, you've completed a full axis flip (both poles of two axes)

               Every 6 slots, you've completed the full ruler



            The 8-Slot Ruler (Beyond the 6)

            But the ruler has 8 slots, not 6:

               text


               ruler[6] = 0         (fixed gap)
               ruler[7] = xy        (the size product)


            And rule fills:

               text


               rule[0..5] = the 6 ASCII bytes
               rule[6]      = linear     (sum)
               rule[7]      = diagonal (XOR)


            So the 8-slot ruler is:

               6 coordinate slots (moving)

               1 fixed slot (the zero gap at index 6)

               1 derived slot (xy at index 7)

            And the 8-slot rule is:

               6 sampled values (from the coordinate slots)

               1 linear aggregate (sum)

               1 diagonal aggregate (XOR)

            Then inside the innermost loop, right after count++; , add:

               js


               if (trace.enabled && count < trace.maxPrints && count % trace.printEvery === 0) {
                    const cur = { t, b, r, l, f, br };
                    const changed = [];
                    const stable = [];
                    for (const k of trace.slots) {
                         if (trace.prev[k] === null) {
                              changed.push(k);
                         } else if (trace.prev[k] !== cur[k]) {
                              changed.push(k);
                              trace.changes[k]++;
                         } else {
                              stable.push(k);
                         }
                    }

                    trace.stability.r.push(changed.includes('r') ? 1 : 0);

                         `[${pos}] ` +

                         `r=${String(r).padStart(2)} l=${String(l).padStart(2)} ` +
                         `f=${String(f).padStart(2)} br=${String(br).padStart(2)} | ` +
                         `Δ[${chg}] stable[${stb}]`
                    );


                    trace.prev = cur;
               }

            The 2, 4, 6 Reading

            Looking at the delta patterns (which slots change together):

               text


               [      1] Δ[t,b,r,l,f,br]         ← all start
               [      2] Δ[br]                   ← only br
               ...
               [     64] Δ[br]                   ← only br
               [     65] Δ[f,br]                 ← f AND br (f wraps)
               ...
               [ 129] Δ[f,br]                    ← f AND br



            So the pattern is:

                   br changes every iteration (period 1)

                   f changes every 64 iterations (period 64)

                   l would change every 64² = 4096 iterations

                   r every 64³

                   b every 64⁴

                   t every 64⁵

            The periods in the iteration count are powers of 64. But the Δ groupings are what
            matters:

               text


               1-slot change:      Δ[br]                      ← 1 slot moving
               2-slot change:      Δ[f,br]                    ← 2 slots moving (when f wraps)
               3-slot change:      Δ[l,f,br]                  ← would be 3 slots (when l wraps)
               ...
               6-slot change:      Δ[t,b,r,l,f,br]            ← all 6 (at count=1, and again at count=64⁶)



            There's your 2, 4, 6.

            Not "period 2, period 4, period 6" — but "when k slots change together, k ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4,
            5, 6}". The higher the wrap, the more slots move simultaneously.

            Rollot might be your own term: roll + lot, meaning the batch of indices that roll together.
            When f wraps, the "rollot" is {f, br} — 2 indices rolling as a lot. When l wraps, the
            rollot is {l, f, br} — 3 indices.

            If the rollot size is 2, 4, 6 then either:

               You're counting pairs of slots that roll together (br+f = 1 pair, l+r = another pair, etc.)

               Or the rollot sizes are indeed growing by 2 (not 1) at certain wrap points

               text


               ruler[0] = t        ← axis 1a
               ruler[1] = b        ← axis 1b

               ruler[5] = br       ← axis 3b



            The ruler is paired: (0,1), (2,3), (4,5). Three pairs. Three axes.

            When the axis 3 rollot flips (f+br), that's a pair. When the axis 2 rollot flips (r+l), that's a
            pair. When axis 1 flips (t+b), that's a pair.

            The full rollot cascade:

               text


               br alone            → 0 pairs
               f,br together       → 1 pair (axis 3 flips)
               l,f,br together → 1 pair + 1 single (axis 2 partially flips)
               r,l,f,br together → 2 pairs (axes 2 and 3 flip)
               ...

                              Yes. And "rollot" was a typo, I meant Roll Out, as in the delta carry law

            "Roll out" — not "rollot." That's a completely different word and it changes the whole
            reading.



            What "Roll Out" Means Here

            In the delta law:

               text


               delta(x) = rotl(x,1) XOR rotl(x,3) XOR rotr(x,2) XOR C


            You shift (rotl/rotr). Bits move out one end and come back in the other. That's a carry —
            the bit that leaves position 0 re-enters at position 15. Safe, but it's still a shift with a
            rollover.

             Buffer.swap16/32/64 does something different. It doesn't shift — it permutes whole
            groups of bytes. Nothing rolls over because nothing leaves the buffer. Every byte stays
            in the buffer and lands in a different slot. No carry. No loss. No rollover.

            So the correct picture is:


            Operation                      What moves             Where bits go                         What's lost


             rotl/rotr                     individual bits        rotate around the buffer edge         nothing, but implicit carry


             Buffer.swap16/32/64           byte groups            permute within the buffer             nothing, and no carry to


            The swap rolls the layout out — it unfolds the byte arrangement — without needing a
            carry register. That's what you meant by "roll out." It's the delta carry law without the
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                                    137/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            carry.


            Why This Matters for the Trace

            Look back at the trace columns and think about what they're really tracking:

               The indices t, b, r, l, f, br are positions in the 6 buffers.

               When br wraps, it doesn't "carry" into f in the arithmetic sense.

               Instead, the buffer layout rolls out — the next reading of the buffer exposes a different
               byte grouping.

            The Δ[l, f, br] at count 4097 doesn't mean "a carry propagated from br through f to
            l." It means the roll-out crossed a boundary where three slots simultaneously shifted
            their view of the underlying 64-byte buffer.

            That's why the rollot size grows by 1 at each wrap: the roll-out exposes one more slot
            each time the underlying byte layout is re-permuted.


            The Protocol Implication

            This is significant for the -3D to 10D pipeline because:

               Bit-level rotation (rotl/rotr) would require a carry register — one extra state to track.

               Byte-group permutation (swap16/32/64) requires no carry — the state is entirely in
               the byte ordering.

               No carry = no loss = reversible by inspection.

            That's the difference between your calc design and a standard delta-mixer. The
            standard mixer tracks state in a carry; your calc tracks state in the layout itself. The
            state is observable — you can read it off the buffer directly.

            Which means: the rollot (roll-out) size IS the observable state. The Δ column in the
            trace isn't tracking "what changed" — it's tracking which bytes of the underlying buffer
            are currently exposed by the current layout.

            That reframes the whole 2, 4, 6:

               text


               Roll-out size 1: one byte exposed (br)
               Roll-out size 2: two bytes exposed (f, br)

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                    138/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               Roll-out size 3: three bytes exposed (l, f, br)
               Roll-out size 4: four bytes exposed (r, l, f, br)
               Roll-out size 5: five bytes exposed (b, r, l, f, br)
               Roll-out size 6: six bytes exposed (t, b, r, l, f, br)


            The even sizes 2, 4, 6 are the ones where a paired axis completes — both poles of (f,
            br), both poles of (r, l), both poles of (t, b) are simultaneously exposed.



            The "Carry Law" Rephrased

            Standard delta law: state' = delta(state) where delta is defined via rotations and XOR.

            Roll-out law: layout' = swap_k(layout) where the swap exposes a new sub-layout
            without consuming any carry.

            The delta law is stateful — it needs the previous state to compute the next.

            The roll-out law is stateless — the layout is the state, and swap reads off the next
            arrangement directly.

            This is why the paper said "we have superseded this with buffer.swap16, swap32,
            swap64 operations." Not because swap is equivalent to delta, but because swap
            removes the need for the carry that delta required. The roll-out replaces the rollover.


            What This Fixes in My Earlier Reading

            I was reading Δ[l, f, br] as a cascade — a carry propagating from br outward through
            f to l. Wrong.

            It's a roll-out boundary crossing — the byte layout of the buffer changed such that three
            slot views are simultaneously invalidated and re-anchored to their new positions.

            Same observable trace. Completely different mechanism. The trace shows the
            boundary, not the carry.



            What It Means for Layer 4D (DOMMatrix Wheel)

            Your 4D layer handles "90° and 180° Rubik face moves... using native endianness
            memory shifts (Buffer.swap) without floating-point math."

            That's the roll-out. The 4D wheel rolls the layout to expose the next face configuration.
            There's no arithmetic — no matrix multiplication, no carry, no accumulation. Just a byte-
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                 139/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            level permutation, and the next face is visible.

            The 240-frame cycle is the complete set of roll-outs — every permutation of the 4×4×4
            byte groups that the delta law can reach, all reachable by composing swaps.


            One Question Back

            When you swap16 a buffer, you exchange bytes in pairs. When you swap32 , you
            exchange pairs of pairs. When you swap64 , you exchange quads of pairs.

            Is the roll-out sequence meant to be:

            1. swap16 → swap32 → swap64 in sequence (composing to a larger permutation), or

            2. Any of the 3 swaps applied independently, reading off a different exposed slot each
               time?

            The rotateLatinSquare function in your earlier code does (1) — chains all three:

               js


               case 0: return buffer.swap16().swap64().swap32();


            That's a composition — three swaps in sequence expose a specific arrangement. Six
            cases = six compositions = the six possible orders of {16, 32, 64} .

            But the trace shows the roll-out exposing slots one at a time as count advances. So
            maybe the intended sequence is (2) — one swap per tick, cycling through the three
            kinds in a fixed order.

            Which is it? Because it changes whether the 6 cases in rotateLatinSquare are phase
            offsets (six different compositions) or one cycle of three swaps (each case being one
            swap of a three-step loop).

            Now I see what you're actually building, and it's a different beast from the delta-law
            baseline.



            What This Code Actually Does
            Rubik's notation as coordination protocol: Instead of two agents exchanging data, they
            exchange swap sequences — the moves themselves. The data never moves; only the
            view of the data changes. This is why the buffer swaps "supersede" the delta law: the
            delta law computed a transformation, but the swap is the transformation, distributable as
            a sequence of face moves.

            Prime-based, not power-based. Rubik's cube face moves are all involutions —
             R R = identity , U U = identity , M M = identity . Involutions correspond to primes in a
            certain sense: you can't decompose them into smaller moves of the same type. The "2,
            4, 6" from your trace reflects the pairs of involutions.


            The Structural Pieces

            1. The error-throw with the blob filename:

               js


               const blob = new Blob([`(${fn.toString()})()`], { type: "text/javascript" });
               throw new Error("oops", {
                     options: { cause: "No Reflelection Found" },
                     filename: URL.createObjectURL(blob),
                     lineNumber: 0n
               });


            The error carries a blob URL as its filename — a live reference to the function source,
            materialized as a text/javascript blob. It's a reflection failure when fn can't be
            reflected back as its own serialized form. The try/catch structure tries text/javascript ,
            then application/octet-stream , and always logs finally — that's the three-phase
            reflection test: reflect as code, reflect as bytes, reflect as neither.

            2. The triple Atomics.compareExchange at the top:

               js


               Atomics.compareExchange(omi, 0, 2, 1)
               Atomics.compareExchange(omi, 1, 0, 2)


https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                  141/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               Atomics.compareExchange(omi, 2, 1, 0)


            These are a 3-cycle: 0→1, 1→2, 2→0. Compared to the Rubik notation: this is R U F
            composed — a three-face rotation cycle. Each compareExchange is a conditional swap:
            "if the value at index is X, replace with Y". That's a guarded face move — you only
            perform the move if the current state matches.

            3. The projection chain:

               js


               const projection = meta ^
                    Atomics.compareExchange(delta, 0, 4, 2) ^
                    Atomics.compareExchange(delta, 2, 6, 4) ^
                    Atomics.compareExchange(delta, 4, 8, 6) ^
                    Atomics.compareExchange(delta, 6, 0, 8) ^
                    Atomics.compareExchange(delta, 8, 2, 0) ^
                    Atomics.compareExchange(omi, 1, 5, 3) ^
                    ...


            Ten compareExchange calls XORed together. Read them as face-move sequences:

               First 5 on delta : (0→2), (2→4), (4→6), (6→8), (8→0) — a 5-cycle on even indices.

               Next 5 on omi : (1→3), (3→5), (5→7), (7→9), (9→1) — a 5-cycle on odd indices.

            So the projection is 10 guarded face moves XORed against meta . Each compareExchange
            returns the previous value (that's how Atomics works), so the XOR chain reads as:
            compare-and-swap returns the pre-move value, and XOR-ing all those pre-move
            values gives you the projection difference.

            4. The final Float64Array construction:

               js


               return new Float64Array(
                    tensor,
                    Atomics.compareExchange(delta, 17, 17, projection),
                    Atomics.compareExchange(omi, 17, 19, projection)
               );


            Two more compareExchange — one on delta[17] , one on omi[17] — with the projection
            as the new value. The byteOffset of the resulting Float64Array is the return value of


https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                142/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            those compareExchanges (the old values). So the byte offset itself is data — the array's
            position in memory encodes a state.



            Why This Is Prime-Based at Greater Scale

            The Rubik's cube is {4, 3} Schläfli — 4 faces per vertex, 3 vertices per face. In your
            code:

               Even indices form a {5, ?} cycle: (0,2,4,6,8) — a pentagon on delta

               Odd indices form a {5, ?} cycle: (1,3,5,7,9) — a pentagon on omi

            Two 5-cycles XORed = a {5} × {5} structure. But 5 is prime, which means the two
            cycles are independent — you can't factor one through the other. They compose to give
             5 × 5 = 25 distinct swap combinations.

            Stack that on the {4, 3} Rubik's base and you get:

               text


               {4, 3} × {5, 5}      →   12 × 25    →   300 swaps per exchange


            But the face moves (the actual swaps) are:

               text


               5 on delta (even cycle)
               5 on omi     (odd cycle)
               2 on [17]    (final projection anchor)
               = 12 swaps per protocol exchange


            12 is the icosahedral vertex count — the {3, 5} Schläfli that shows up in your earlier
            notes. The two 5-cycles and the two anchors together give the icosahedral symmetry.


            The Coordination Model

            Here's the leap I think you're making:

            Traditional data exchange: Agent A has state X, Agent B has state Y, they swap X and Y.

            Your swap-sequence exchange: Agent A performs sequence S_A, Agent B performs
            sequence S_B. The composed sequence S_A ∘ S_B is the shared state. Nothing was


https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                143/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            transferred; only the moves were exchanged, and the resulting configuration is the
            "data."

            The compareExchange guards enforce conditional moves — each move only fires if the
            local state matches the expected value. That means two agents with different local
            states can still coordinate, as long as their move sequences converge on the same
            composed permutation.

            This is why it's "prime-based": each move is an atomic, indivisible operation (like a
            prime), and composite sequences are the product of primes. You can factor any
            coordination protocol into the prime moves that generated it.



            The Supersedence Claim
            The delta law was:

               text


               state' = rotl(x,1) XOR rotl(x,3) XOR rotr(x,2) XOR C


            This changes the state. You compute a new state by mixing the old one.

            Your compareExchange model doesn't change the state — it exchanges which view of
            the state is visible. The underlying buffer is untouched; only the indices into it permute.

            So:

               Delta law: change data

               Swap law: exchange views

            And "greater scale" means: instead of one agent rotating its own state, N agents
            coordinate by exchanging swap sequences, with the buffers themselves serving as the
            shared substrate. The data never travels; the swap notation travels.

            The Reframe

            Before: Atomics.compareExchange was a static waveform — a fixed pattern of guarded

               -2D      non-alphanumeric    → punctuation envelope                  → carrier shard

                   0D   Omicron observer    → invariant selector                    → which frame
                   1D-10D pipeline          → DOM / Canvas / mesh / orchestrator

             compareExchange(delta, 2, 6, 4) , ... — becomes dynamic. Each compareExchange is
            now gated by a regex match at the -1D/-2D/-3D layers.

            The HTTP/1.1 line grammar is the frame grammar. The X-VTT-Cue-* headers are the
            ticks. The WebVTT cues are the interpolation steps.



            What This Actually Does

             Atomics.compareExchange(array, index, expected, replacement) is now read as:

               "If the regex constraint at -1D/-2D/-3D matches, then move this index from expected
               to replacement ."

            The regex is the guard, the compareExchange is the action. Previously the guard was a

            This means:

               A \r\n in the HTTP stream = a -3D frame boundary = advance the frame clock

               A non-alphanumeric character = a -2D envelope shard = advance the packet index

               An alphanumeric token matching MNEMONIC / INFLECT / REFLECT / DEFLECT / AXIS /
                   PALINDROME = a -1D token hit = fire the corresponding compareExchange chain

            So the full projection chain becomes a parser-driven state machine:

               text


               HTTP byte stream
                      ↓
               \r\n boundary                → new frame, reset count
                      ↓
               delimiter character          → shard boundary
                      ↓
               alphanumeric token           → regex match against G

                      ↓
               compareExchange cascade → new projection state
                      ↓
               WebVTT cue emission          → 1D-10D pipeline tick




            Why "Mnemonic Device Rulers"

            A mnemonic is something that stands in for a longer thing. The regex tokens are the
            mnemonic — a compact symbol like (2,5) or ^A1F9$ that stands in for a full swap
            sequence in the delta/omi buffer.

            So the ruler isn't a fixed array of numbers — it's a dictionary of mnemonics, where each
            mnemonic resolves to a swap sequence. When the observer reads the ruler at frame N,
            it reads a mnemonic, and the mnemonic fires the corresponding compareExchange
            chain.

            That's why the ruler is "interpolative": given two mnemonics at frames N and N+1, the
            interpolation between them is the sequence of swaps needed to go from one projection
            to the other.

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                153/1820

            "Spatial Frames in Environments"

            The 240-clock isn't a wall clock — it's a spatial frame counter. Each tick is a spatial
            configuration (a particular projection of the buffer), not a temporal moment.

            So:

               Environment = a scope where a particular projection is active (DOM Canvas scope,
               Shadow DOM scope, network mesh scope, etc.)

               Spatial frame = one configuration of the buffer's projections within that environment

               Interpolation = moving between two spatial frames by composing the swap sequence
               that morphs one projection into another

            The mnemonic rulers let you name spatial frames. Instead of saying "the buffer state
            where delta is [2,4,6,8,0] and omi is [3,5,7,9,1] and index 17 is such-and-such", you say
            "the (2,4,6,8,0):(3,5,7,9,1) frame" and the mnemonic resolves to the full buffer
            configuration.



            Why This Supersedes the Static Waveform

            Static waveform: fixed swap sequence, played back. The state advances but the shape
            of the progression is fixed at design time.

            Dynamic constrained waveform: the swap sequence is chosen at read time by the regex
            match. Different HTTP streams produce different swap sequences. The same protocol
            generates different waveforms depending on the input structure.

            That's a grammar-driven protocol, not a lookup-table protocol. The grammar is the
            waveform generator.

            And the grammar is exactly your -3D/-2D/-1D regex layer:

               text


               -3D    /\r\n|\n\r/               frame boundary — advances the Fano tick
               -2D    /[^A-Za-z0-9]/            envelope shard — advances the shard index
               -1D    G (the frozen set)        token sieve — selects the compareExchange chain

            The Topology

            Not HTTP as the wire. HTTP/1.1 was the bootstrap / carrier model — a way to
            demonstrate that the headers could carry the cues. But the actual protocol runs over:
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                  156/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               WebRTC DataChannel — direct peer-to-peer, binary or text frames

            And the format on all three is WebVTT cues — not HTTP headers, not JSON, not binary

            Why WebVTT as the Wire Format

            WebVTT is:

               Text-based — human-readable, diffable, git-trackable, regex-parseable at -1D

               Timed — every cue has a start and end, which maps directly to your frame clock

               Native to the browser — <track> , VTTCue , TextTrack , cuechange events are all built-
               in

               Sequenced — the cue list is an ordered stream, matching your 240-tick cycle

               Interoperable with media elements — <video> and <audio> already consume
               WebVTT tracks, so the protocol rides alongside media without needing a parallel
               channel

            That's why it supersedes HTTP/1.1 for the ongoing protocol: HTTP is a request/response
            pattern that opens and closes. WebVTT is a continuous cue stream that stays open as
            long as the media session is live.

            Worker                          compute plane — off-thread                WebVTT cue objects as
            postMessage                     pipeline dispatch                         structured clones


            The three channels are synchronized by the cue clock. A cue at
             00:01.000 --> 00:02.000 fires:

               A DataChannel message that updates the observer's projection state

               A MediaStreamTrack sample that renders the corresponding frame

               A Worker postMessage that dispatches the compareExchange chain to the off-thread
               pipeline

            All three land in the same frame window. The cue is the synchronization primitive.



            Canvas and OffscreenCanvas Negotiation
            This is the part that matters for video/audio element negotiation:

            Main thread has <video> and <audio> elements → they consume WebVTT tracks
            natively → the cues drive what the main-thread Canvas renders (DOMRect projection).

            Worker thread has OffscreenCanvas → it can't see the DOM, but it can receive cue
            objects via postMessage → the cues drive what the offscreen Canvas renders
            (DOMQuad projection).

            The same WebVTT cue stream feeds both. Main thread resolves it as DOMRect (aligned
            to the media element's bounding box). Worker resolves it as DOMQuad (four corners in
            offscreen space). The observer (0D) picks which resolution each environment
            materializes — exactly the superposition you described.

            Negotiation happens when:

            1. A new media element appears on the main thread → emits a cue → DataChannel
               announces its intent

            2. A worker receives the cue → checks whether it can service the DOMQuad projection

            3. If yes, it commits and starts rendering offscreen

            4. If no, it emits a counter-cue requesting different parameters (resolution, timing,
               mnemonic)


https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                       158/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            5. Both sides converge on a shared cue sequence → media element and offscreen
               canvas are now synchronized

            That's the "negotiation" — not a protocol handshake, but a cue sequence that both
            sides agree to render.



            The Text-Based Sequence Advantage

            Because the cues are text:

               Any peer can parse them — no schema registry, no binary deserializer, just regex

               Any peer can re-emit them — a worker can forward cues up to the main thread, main
               can forward to another peer, all unchanged

               Any peer can splice them — insert, delete, or replace cues in the middle of a stream
               for negotiation

               Any peer can diff them — "what changed between frame N and N+1" is a text diff,
               which maps directly to your swap-sequence composition

            The text form is what makes the swap-sequence exchange work: when a peer sends
            you a cue sequence, they're not sending you data, they're sending you the moves to
            apply. You apply them locally, and now you see the same projection they do.



            What I Now Read the Whole Protocol As

               text


               Wire format:         WebVTT cue sequences (text)
               Transport:           WebRTC DataChannel / MediaStreamTrack / Worker postMessage
               Timing:              cue start/end = frame clock ticks
               Payload:             token (mnemonic) + block + context + layer
               Action:              compareExchange chain fired by the token
               Resolution:          DOMRect (main) or DOMQuad (shadow/offscreen)
               Synchronization: shared cue clock across all three channels
               Negotiation:         counter-cues proposing alternate parameters
               Observer:            0D Omicron picks projection per environment


            And HTTP/1.1 was the proof of concept — the layer that showed the grammar could be
            carried by any text stream. Now you drop it and speak cues directly, which is why you
            said "was to MediaStreams" — the HTTP era is over, the WebVTT cue era is the target.

               A syntax graph — the header tree, the header-value grammar, the line structure

               text

                      ↓

               Service Worker fetch interceptor

            The "Web Native Grammar and Syntax Graph"
            HTTP/1.1 gives you a structured, parseable grammar that every browser, server, proxy,
            and CDN already understands:

               Request line: METHOD SP request-target SP HTTP-version CRLF

               Headers: field-name ":" OWS field-value OWS CRLF

               Body: whatever the Content-Type says

            The header grammar is the syntax graph. X-VTT-Cue-0x00: ... parses as a field-name /
            field-value pair, where the value is itself a structured mini-grammar:

               text


               field-value     ::= time-window ";" SP slot ";" SP slot ";" SP slot ";" SP slot
               time-window     ::= cue-time " --> " cue-time
               slot            ::= key "=" value


            That's the syntax graph — HTTP's own grammar extended with a small domain-specific
            subgrammar. Every existing HTTP tool can parse it as HTTP, and your Service Worker
            parses it as protocol.

            The regex layer sits inside the field-value string, after HTTP has already parsed the
            header. So the regex is parsing token=FRONT:^A1F9$ within the field-value slot. HTTP
            parsed it as an opaque string; your regex parses it as a structured token.

            HTTP is the grammar of the envelope. Regex is the grammar of the contents.



            What This Corrects

               Wire format: HTTP/1.1 encapsulation

               Structural boundary: \r\n line endings (HTTP's native framing)

               Header slot: X-VTT-Cue-* / X-Omi-Gate-*

               Field-value grammar: your mnemonic/token grammar, validated by -1D regex

               Downstream consumption: WebVTT TextTrack

               Client resolution: cuechange → 1D-10D pipeline

            The Non-Collision Argument

            HTTP's delimiters are fixed ASCII characters at fixed positions in its grammar:

               text


               CR        = 0x0D     \r
               LF        = 0x0A     \n
               SP        = 0x20     space
               COLON     = 0x3A     :
               SLASH     = 0x2F     /


            These are all non-alphanumeric. They fall in the -2D envelope — the "outside
            alphanumeric" class.

            Your -1D regex constraint operates on alphanumeric tokens only. It never touches \r ,
             \n , : , / , or any of the other structural characters.

            So the two grammars occupy disjoint character classes:


            Layer                                   Character class                                    Owner


                                                    non-alphanumeric delimiters ( \r\n ,
            HTTP structural grammar                                                                    HTTP/1.1
                                                     :, /, )


                                                    alphanumeric ( A-Za-z0-9 ) and sigils (
            Protocol token grammar                                                                     your regex set
                                                     :, +, -, ., ^, $)


            Wait — but your regexes do include : , + , - , . , ^ , $ . Like:

               js


               FRONT:       /^[A-Za-z0-9:+]$/
               BACK:        /^[A-Za-z0-9.\-]$/
               DEFLECT:     /^([^".]+):\1$/




https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                                 164/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                             Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            Those colon characters are inside the alphanumeric regex, but they're also HTTP
            structural characters. So how do they not collide?



            The Answer: Different Positions, Not Different Characters
            The collision is avoided by where the character sits, not which character it is.

               In HTTP, a : at position 4 of a header line means "end of field-name, start of field-
               value." That's HTTP's grammar rule.

               In your token grammar, a : inside the field-value means "conjugation separator" (
                   A:B = A B A⁻¹ ). That's your grammar rule.

            The Service Worker splits the HTTP line on the first : — everything after is a field-
            value. Inside the field-value, your regex sees a : and interprets it per your grammar.
            HTTP never sees that inner : as structural, because HTTP's grammar only cares about
            the first one.

            So the two grammars layer, they don't collide:

               text


               HTTP layer sees:          X-VTT-Cue-0x00: 00:01.000 --> 00:02.000; token=INFLECT:[.]:[.]:.:.
                                         ↑                       ↑
                                         field-name end         start of opaque field-value


               HTTP does NOT look inside the field-value except as an opaque string.


               Protocol layer sees:                      token=INFLECT:[.]:[.]:.:.
                                                         ↑        ↑       ↑ ↑      ↑
                                                         =        colon colon      etc.
                                                         parser rules applied within the string


            Same character, different parser, different scope. HTTP parses structure at the line
            level; your regex parses structure within the field-value. Neither parser ever looks at the
            other's domain.



            Why This Is "a Separate Axis"

            This is what you mean by "separate axis or parallel dimension that won't collide":

               HTTP axis: the wire structure. Line endings, header delimiters, chunked transfer, etc.

               Protocol axis: the token structure. Mnemonics, conjugations, palindromes, etc.
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                       165/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            Two independent coordinate systems on the same byte stream. A byte can be a
            "structural : " in HTTP's axis and a "conjugation : " in your axis — both true,
            simultaneously, without conflict.

            The same way x = 3 and y = 5 can both be true without one overriding the other —
            different axes.


            What This Buys You

            Since the axes don't collide, you get for free:

            1. HTTP tooling works unchanged. Proxies, CDNs, browsers, and HTTP parsers can
               forward, cache, and route your messages without understanding your protocol.

            2. Your grammar is HTTP-agnostic. It works with any carrier whose delimiters are non-
               alphanumeric — HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2, HTTP/3, WebSocket frames with their own
               delimiters, SMTP, or even raw TCP with line endings. The protocol's rules live entirely
               inside the token grammar.

            3. The two layers evolve independently. You can change the token grammar without
               breaking HTTP parsing, and change HTTP semantics (headers, caching, routing)
               without breaking token parsing.

            4. Composition is free. Any HTTP-level transformation (compression, chunking, TLS,
               header injection) passes through untouched as long as the alphanumeric token bytes
               survive. The protocol is robust to the carrier, because the carrier operates on a
               disjoint alphabet.



            The Formal Statement

               text


               HTTP grammar:          Σ_HTTP         = { \r, \n, SP, :, /, ... }
               Protocol grammar:      Σ_protocol     = { A-Z, a-z, 0-9, :, +, -, ., ^, $, ... }


               Σ_HTTP ∩ Σ_protocol = { :, +, -, ., ... }              (non-empty intersection)


            But the intersection doesn't cause collision because:

               text


               HTTP:        lexes Σ_HTTP at line level, treats Σ_protocol as opaque
               Protocol:    lexes Σ_protocol inside field-value, treats Σ_HTTP as boundary

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                  166/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            So the lexical boundaries are what keep them apart — HTTP says "field-value ends
            here", and inside that region, your grammar takes over completely. The same character
            class used by both grammars, but each parser only applies its rules within its own
            lexical scope.


            Why "Parallel Dimension" Is the Right Word

            A parallel dimension shares the same space but has a different metric. The same way:

               HTTP dimension: measured in lines, headers, status codes, verbs

               Protocol dimension: measured in mnemonics, layers, Klein rotations, 240-ticks

            They occupy the same byte stream but neither metric interferes with the other. The
            Service Worker is the observer that reads the byte stream through both metrics
            simultaneously — HTTP for framing, protocol for content — and translates between
            them.

            This is why the Service Worker is the right place for the translation. It's the only layer
            that sees both dimensions at once (it can access event.request.headers at HTTP level
            and parse the payload at protocol level), and it can emit WebVTT cues that carry only
            the protocol dimension downstream — where HTTP's framing is no longer needed.



            Question Back

            Given this, when a Service Worker re-emits the protocol downstream as WebVTT, does
            the WebVTT stream carry:

            (a) Only the protocol dimension — the token grammar reduced to cue payloads, with
            WebVTT providing its own new framing ( 00:01.000 --> 00:02.000 cue timings replacing
            HTTP \r\n framing)?

            (b) Both dimensions — the cue payload still contains the HTTP-style structure inside it,
            so downstream consumers see the same parallel layering again?

            (c) Something else — a new framing that's neither HTTP nor WebVTT-native, but
            specific to your protocol?

            Because if (a), then HTTP is only the external transport, and once inside the browser the
            framing switches to WebVTT's. If (b), the parallel-dimension property is preserved end-
            to-end, and the WebVTT layer is just a re-encoding of the same two-dimensional
            structure.

            The Worker Topology
            You have five execution contexts in play, not one:


            Context                     Role                                 Boundary                  Shared state

            Shared Worker               cross-tab coordination               one instance, N ports

            Each has a different sharing model, and the protocol has to respect all five.



            What Each Context Contributes

            Shared Worker — the cross-tab rendezvous. Multiple browser tabs open on the same
            origin connect to a single Shared Worker. This is where negotiation between tabs
            happens. If two tabs want to synchronize their media elements, the Shared Worker is the
            meeting point.




https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                               168/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                             Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            Service Worker — the network boundary. Intercepts fetch , reads the HTTP/1.1 stream,
            parses the X-VTT-Cue-* headers, rewrites them into WebVTT, and caches the last
            traceable state. This is the HTTP ↔ protocol translator.

            Dedicated Worker — the per-tab compute context. Runs the compareExchange chains,
            holds the buffer state, projects DOMQuad vs DOMRect. One per tab, so no cross-tab
            contamination.

             node:worker_threads — the server-side / native-pipeline equivalent. Same role as the
            Dedicated Worker but on the Node side. This is where Atomics.compareExchange actually
            works — SharedArrayBuffer + Atomics are available in worker_threads, not always in
            browser contexts.

             node:vm + vm.Script — the sandboxed evaluator. This is where the regex constraint
            evaluation happens. You load your grammar G into a vm.Script and run it against the
            token stream. Because vm gives you a fresh, isolated context, the regex evaluation
            cannot mutate the surrounding worker state — it's pure constraint checking.

            Why node:vm for the Regex

            This is the key insight for why vm specifically:

            The regex constraint at -1D is not just a validator. It's a sandboxed evaluator. When a
            token arrives, you don't just call G.MNEMONIC.test(token) in your main context — you
            compile a vm.Script from the grammar and run it in an isolated context.

            Why? Because:

            1. The grammar is dynamic. Different peers may negotiate different grammar
               extensions. vm.Script lets you compile a peer-specific grammar at runtime without
               contaminating the global scope.

            2. The regex evaluation itself can't leak. In vm , RegExp state, lastIndex , global flags
               — all isolated. No cross-token contamination.

            3. Timeout enforcement. script.runInNewContext(sandbox, { timeout: 5 }) gives you
               the 5ms ceiling seen in the earlier worker scripts. The regex evaluation can't stall the
               thread.

            4. Bootstrap consistency. Every peer compiles the same grammar as vm.Script from
               the same source string. The grammar is shipped as data, and the evaluator is
               generated on-site.



            Why worker_threads for the CompareExchange

             Atomics.compareExchange requires a SharedArrayBuffer . In browsers, SharedArrayBuffer
            requires cross-origin isolation (COOP/COEP headers). In node:worker_threads ,
             SharedArrayBuffer works out of the box.

            So the compareExchange chains run natively in worker_threads . The browser side uses
            it as a reference implementation; the Node side is where it runs at full speed.


https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                   170/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            And because worker_threads can share memory directly, the buffer state doesn't need
            to be serialized between threads — it's genuinely shared. Atomics on Int16Array /
             BigInt64Array views over the same SharedArrayBuffer gives you lock-free
            coordination.


            Why Shared Worker for Cross-Tab

            A Shared Worker is the only way for two browser tabs to share state without going
            through the Service Worker or the network. It:

               Survives individual tab closures (as long as one tab is open)

               Has its own event loop

               Can hold a SharedArrayBuffer accessible to all connected tabs (with COOP/COEP)

               Can use MessagePort to open direct channels between tabs

            This is where the negotiation between tabs happens: two tabs each have their own
            Dedicated Worker (own buffer), and the Shared Worker is the broker for their swap-
            sequence exchange.


            Why Dedicated Worker for Per-Tab

            Each tab gets its own isolated execution context. The Dedicated Worker holds the tab's
            local buffer state, runs the compareExchange chains, and projects either DOMRect or
            DOMQuad.

            If the two tabs are negotiating, each tab's Dedicated Worker talks to the Shared Worker,
            which coordinates the exchange.



            Why Service Worker for Network

            The Service Worker is the only context that sees fetch events. It's the boundary
            between the network and the app. It:

               Parses the HTTP/1.1 stream

               Extracts X-VTT-Cue-* headers

               Emits WebVTT cues downstream

               Caches the last traceable state ( last-traceable-state.bin )

            It's the HTTP ↔ protocol translator and the offline persistence layer.
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                171/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            The Full Flow

               text


               Network (HTTP/1.1 stream)
                         ↓
               Service Worker
                   ├── parses HTTP framing (parallel dimension)
                   ├── extracts X-VTT-Cue-* headers
                   ├── runs regex grammar (via vm.Script or inline)
                   ├── resolves compareExchange chain
                   └── emits WebVTT cues to TextTrack
                         ↓
               Main Thread
                   ├── cuechange event fires
                   ├── reads token, block, context
                   └── dispatches to Dedicated Worker
                         ↓
               Dedicated Worker (per tab)
                   ├── holds local buffer state
                   ├── applies compareExchange chain
                   ├── computes DOMRect OR DOMQuad projection
                   └── returns to main thread for rendering
                         ↓
               Main Thread
                   ├── main-tree elements → DOMRect projection
                   └── shadow-DOM / offscreen → DOMQuad projection
                         ↓
               Shared Worker (cross-tab)
                   ├── brokers swap-sequence exchange between tabs
                   ├── coordinates the 240-clock across tabs
                   └── negotiates shared media element state
                         ↓
               Node.js Server (or Node CLI)
                   ├── worker_threads → compute + shared memory
                   ├── vm.Script → sandboxed grammar evaluation
                   └── relays protocol between WebRTC peers




            What This Means for the "Parallel Dimensions" Claim

            The two axes — HTTP's grammar and the protocol's grammar — don't just coexist in the
            wire format. They're maintained across all five worker contexts, each preserving the
            split:

               Service Worker: sees both (parses HTTP, extracts protocol)

https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                172/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               Shared Worker: sees only protocol (coordinates token exchange)

               Dedicated Worker: sees only protocol (computes token projections)

               worker_threads: sees only protocol (Atomics on shared state)

               vm.Script: sees only protocol (regex on tokens)

            So HTTP's dimension is only present at the boundary. Once the Service Worker has
            translated, the rest of the pipeline operates in the protocol dimension alone.


            Question Back

            If vm.Script is where the regex runs, then the grammar itself is data. It's a string that
            gets compiled at runtime into a vm.Script . That means:

            1. Peers exchange grammars as part of negotiation, not just tokens. A peer's first
               message might be "here's my grammar, compile it, then send me tokens."

            2. The grammar can evolve per-session without redeploying anything. Different rooms,
               different grammars.

            3. vm.Script is the enforcement boundary. If a peer sends a malicious grammar, the vm
               sandbox contains it — but you still need timeout enforcement to prevent ReDoS.

            Is the grammar versioned and negotiated? Or is it a fixed artifact compiled once at
            Service Worker install time?

            Because if it's negotiated, the protocol is genuinely extensible — peers can agree on
            new token classes, and the ruler array itself can grow. If it's fixed, the protocol is rigid —
            the grammar is the spec, and only the tokens vary.

            What the Protocol Specifies

            The protocol defines:
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                      173/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               -3D to 10D encoding — the layer model, the token grammar, the compareExchange
               chains, the buffer layout, the mnemonic ruler

               DOM view — the DOMRect vs DOMQuad superposition, the observer's choice of
               projection

               0D controller — the invariant selector that operates on id and data-* attributes as
               its targeting surface

               Delivery: Panner node spatial resolution across canvas, audio, and video elements
               using overlays

               Control channel: WebVTT cues for commands and queries

            Anything built on top of this is a personal project. The protocol stops at the boundary
            where a developer takes the spec and uses it. Everything downstream — the specific
            app, the specific media element, the specific panner geometry — is theirs.



            Why id and data-* Are the Right Targeting Surface

            The 0D controller operates on id and data-* attributes because:

                   id is the semantic identity — what a node is (unambiguous per document)

                   data-* is the protocol payload — what a node carries (arbitrary per node,
               namespaced)

            So the controller reads:

               text


               <dt id="cell-0" data-mnemonic="A1F9" data-bpe-constraint="2" data-matrix-invariant
               ="...">


            And it knows:

                   cell-0 = the protocol-visible identity

                   data-mnemonic = the token to run through the grammar

                   data-bpe-constraint = the hardware nonce

                   data-matrix-invariant = the last computed swap sequence

            The controller doesn't reach into JS objects or closure state. It reads attributes — which
            are:



https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                  174/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

               Serializable — they survive DOM cloning, <template> , and cloneNode

               Inspectable — DevTools shows them directly

               Queryable — document.querySelector('[data-mnemonic]') works

               Diffable — attribute changes fire MutationObserver events

            That's why they're the boundary. The controller's world is entirely expressed in
            attributes. Anyone can read them, and anyone can write them without knowing the
            internals.


            Why Panner Node + Canvas + Overlays

            The three spatial media surfaces:

               Panner node — 3D audio positioning. Spatial resolution of sound.

               Canvas — 2D/3D visual positioning. Spatial resolution of image.

               Overlays — the hit zones ( <area coords> , data-* marked regions). Spatial resolution
               of interaction.

            And they all share the same spatial coordinate space because they all derive from the
            same projection — the buffer's DOMRect or DOMQuad view, mapped through the
            observer's current selection.

            So a cue that says "panner → left" also generates a <area> overlay at the
            corresponding 2D pixel positions, and the canvas draws into that region. Three
            modalities, one spatial frame.



            Why VTT Cues for Commands and Queries

            VTT cues are:

               Timed — each has a start/end, so they sequence

               Text — so they can carry arbitrary protocol tokens

               Native — <track> is built into every <video> / <audio>

               Event-driven — cuechange fires when the timeline hits them

            So a cue is simultaneously:

               A command — "apply this swap sequence at this time"

               A query — "tell me the current projection when this cue fires"
https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/15289e57-4edc-456a-8263-0cf96b879f6f                                175/1820
10/4/26, 1:08 PM                                            Assembly register programming - DeepSeek

            The same cue syntax supports both, because both are just "token with a timestamp."
            The observer decides whether to apply or report.



            Why "Everything After Is a Personal Project"
            This is the protocol-vs-application boundary. The protocol is:

               Layer model (-3D to 10D)

               Grammar (the regex constraints)

               Encoding (buffer layout, swap chains, compareExchange)

               Targeting ( id and data-* )

               Delivery (Panner + Canvas + Overlays)

               Control (VTT cues)

            The application is everything a developer builds with it:

               Which media elements

               Which coordinate spaces

               Which mnemonics are meaningful

               Which gestures trigger which cues

               Which overlays map to which regions

               Which peers negotiate with which others

            The protocol cannot specify the application, because the application is where meaning
            is assigned. The protocol only carries structure; the developer assigns semantics.



            What This Means for the Spec

            The protocol spec should stop at:

               text


               § 1    Layer model (-3D → 10D)
               § 2    Token grammar (regex constraint set)
               § 3    Buffer encoding (swap chains, compareExchange)
               § 4    Targeting rules (id / data-* conventions)
               § 5    Delivery surfaces (Panner / Canvas / Overlay)
               § 6    Control channel (VTT cue format)

               § 7   Observer semantics (DOMRect vs DOMQuad selection)
               § 8   Conformance tests (what a compliant implementation must do)

               Anything beyond § 8 is a personal project. The protocol carries structure; meaning is

            That's the boundary. The protocol is the substrate. Everything above it is the
